Therefore, the leaching reaction between anode materials and HCl solution is a multiple process (Aurbach et al., 2002; Jiangfeng et al., 2004): (1) the hydrolysis of lithium salt (2) double decomposition reaction between lithium salt and HCl solution.",

        "evidence_hint": "Fig. 9 shows the alteration of the leaching efficiency of Li as a function of HCl - H2O2 ratio in the range of 1:0–25:1 by using HCl concentration of 3 M, S/L of 1:50, leaching time of 90 min and leaching temperature of 80 ^∘C. It can be observed from Fig. 7 that the leaching efficiency of Li was 97.1% without H2O2. And it reached to 99.4% with HCl - H2O2 ration being 10:1. A conclusion can be drawn from the experiment that H2O2 had a slight role in the leaching process of Li. In the leaching process for Hydrometallurgy, H2O2 was added as reductant to convert undissolved high valence ions to soluble low ones to promote the reaction process. Because of the absence of intermediate valence of Li, H2O2 can reduce Co(III) to Co (II) and thus facilitate the forward reaction. The result shows that the amount of H2O2 was sufficient for the complete dissolution of lithium, as further increasing the concentration of H2O2 did not show a significant effect in the recovery efficiency compared to consumption of expensive of lithium. Considering lower energy consumption and relatively better leaching efficiency, the suitable leaching agent was HCl without H2O2.",

Characterization of the anode active materials in spent LIBs\n\nThe characterization of anode active materials in spent LIBs was firstly analyzed with an X-ray diffractometer. As shown in Fig. 2, the strong diffraction intensity of peaks appeared at 2h of 26.5\u0001, in correspondence with the characteristic peaks of the hexagonal structure (PDF#25-284), indicated that the main composition of anode active materials was graphite with integrated graphite layered structure. And from the figure, it can be noted that the graphite materials basically remain good crystallinity and the complete layer structure after electrochemical processes.\n\nFig. 3 shows the SEM images of the anode active materials of spent LIBs. It confirms that the structure of the graphite materials has no change after the charge and discharge cycles. The graphite particles were not smooth and covered with sticky substances. Combined with the compositions and the characteristics of the anode matter, it can be inferred that the sticky substances are composed of organic electrolytes, plasticizer and the polyvinylidene fluoride (PVDF) binders.\n\nThese adhesive agents, polyvinylidene fluoride (PVDF) binders in this study, have good chemical stability and mechanical performance, making it difficult to react with most of the strong acid, that can cause some issues during solid–liquid separation after\n\nleaching and lower the recovery efficiency of the process. Therefore, it is usual to implement such pyrolysis pretreatment before hydrometallurgical processes as the high temperature decomposes the organic compounds in the electrodes, i.e. binder and carbon (Sun and Qiu, 2011, 2012).\n\nIn order to decompose them effectively, thermogravimetric analysis (DTG) and differential thermal analysis (DTA) of anode were carried to determine the pyrolysis temperature range as shown in Fig. 4. Initially is observed a weight decrease (115 ^∘𝖢) due to loss of water in the anode active materials. With the temperature increase to 490 ^∘𝖢, it happened significantly weight loss and the maximum weight loss rate of anode active materials was in 590 ^∘C. In the meantime, the oxycompound groups in organic matters were degraded completely remaining the graphite with stable chemical properties. Therefore, in the experiment, the anode active powder materials separated from the spent batteries were calcined at 600 \u0001C for 1 h in a muffle and then cooled to room temperature to degrade organic compounds.\n\nTable 1 shows the metal compositions of the anode active materials. From the table, it can be noted that except tiny amounts of Co, Ni and Fe, the main metals contained in the powder materials are Cu and Li. Especially Li, its content is 30.07 mg/g, much higher than that of the environmental standards. The high content of lithium maybe come from two aspects.\n\n[TABLE:TABLE_Leaching Lithium from the Anode Electrode Materials of Spent Lithium-Ion Batteries by Hydrochloric Acid (Hcl)_0001]\n\n(1) In the process of lithium-ion travelling between the anode electrode and cathode electrode for energy storage and release, parts of the lithium-ion were inserted into the pores of graphite (Filso et al., 2013).\n\n(2) The solid electrolyte interface (SEI) film was found to be stuck to the interface of graphite and electrolyte by interfacial reaction. The main compositions of the SEI film were inorganic constituents (including Li2CO3, LiF, Li O) and organic constituents (including ROCO Li, ROLi, (ROCO Li) ) Xiaoxing, 2013.\n\nReturn exactly:\n{\n  \"decision\": \"bind\" or \"keep_table_entity\",\n  \"entity_id\": \"one candidate id\" or null,\n  \"sample_state\": \"short state description or null\",\n  \"evidence_quote\": \"short exact quote from Local study evidence\"\n}",

        "description": "Fig. 9 shows the alteration of the leaching efficiency of Li as a function of HCl - H2O2 ratio in the range of 1:0–25:1 by using HCl concentration of 3 M, S/L of 1:50, leaching time of 90 min and leaching temperature of 80 ^∘C. It can be observed from Fig. 7 that the leaching efficiency of Li was 97.1% without H2O2. And it reached to 99.4% with HCl - H2O2 ration being 10:1. A conclusion can be drawn from the experiment that H2O2 had a slight role in the leaching process of Li. In the leaching process for Hydrometallurgy, H2O2 was added as reductant to convert undissolved high valence ions to soluble low ones to promote the reaction process. Because of the absence of intermediate valence of Li, H2O2 can reduce Co(III) to Co (II) and thus facilitate the forward reaction. The result shows that the amount of H2O2 was sufficient for the complete dissolution of lithium, as further increasing the concentration of H2O2 did not show a significant effect in the recovery efficiency compared to consumption of expensive of lithium.           "comparison_evidence": {
            "value": 84,
            "unit": "%",
            "treatment": "deionized water only",
            "evidence_span": "As shown in Fig. 5, leaching efficiency was firstly increasing and then decreasing with the continuous increasing of acid concentration. At the first stage, the hydrolysis of lithium salt was dominated that caused leaching efficiency can reach 83.6% when only using deionized water as the leachate merely (see Eqs. (1)–(3)). After adding HCl, it increased from 84% to 98% with HCl concentration increasing from 2.5 to 3 M. This is because that the increasing acid concentration brings incremental amount of proton which can replace more Li+. On the other hand, when HCl was consumed at the solid liquid interphase, the concentration gradient of H+ between solid–liquid interface and solution was gradually increased. It leaded to a quicker diffusion velocity. Both of them can promote the leaching process and bring higher leaching efficiency. Henceforth, the leaching reaction reached equilibrium, and the leaching efficiency of lithium reached its maximum. This is because that the diffusion velocity would not change with further increase of HCl concentration, and the Li+ had been replaced sufficiently. From the figure, 3 M was employed as the optimum acid concentration."
          }

        "id": "REL_reviewed_input_Process_reviewed_c2ca1a770bfa07",
        "source_id": "Component_anode_active_material",
        "target_id": "Process_reviewed_c2ca1a770bfa07",
        "source_name": "anode active material",
        "target_name": "deionized water leaching of calcined anode powder",
        "relation_type": "PROCESSED_BY",
        "evidence_span": "As described above, the main forms of Li in the anode active material are Li2CO3,, Li O, LiF, ROCO Li, CH OLi. Because some of them are water-soluble, such as ROCO2 Li CH3OLi Li2O,, in the experiment performed it has a high leaching efficiency of lithium (84%) only in deionized water. While others are almost insoluble in water and inlaid in the anode active materials, such as ROCO Li, LiF, and they will occur decomposition reaction in HCl solution. Therefore, the leaching reaction between anode materials and HCl solution is a multiple process (Aurbach et al., 2002; Jiangfeng et al., 2004): (1) the hydrolysis of lithium salt (2) double decomposition reaction between lithium salt and HCl solution.",
        "properties": {
          "study_scope": "current_study",
          "graph_status": "active",
          "source_sample": "calcined anode powder",
          "evidence_span": "As described above, the main forms of Li in the anode active material are Li2CO3,, Li O, LiF, ROCO Li, CH OLi. Because some of them are water-soluble, such as ROCO2 Li CH3OLi Li2O,, in the experiment performed it has a high leaching efficiency of lithium (84%) only in deionized water. While others are almost insoluble in water and inlaid in the anode active materials, such as ROCO Li, LiF, and they will occur decomposition reaction in HCl solution. Therefore, the leaching reaction between anode materials and HCl solution is a multiple process (Aurbach et al., 2002; Jiangfeng et al., 2004): (1) the hydrolysis of lithium salt (2) double decomposition reaction between lithium salt and HCl solution."
        }

        "evidence_span": "Fig. 9 shows the alteration of the leaching efficiency of Li as a function of HCl - H2O2 ratio in the range of 1:0–25:1 by using HCl concentration of 3 M, S/L of 1:50, leaching time of 90 min and leaching temperature of 80 ^∘C. It can be observed from Fig. 7 that the leaching efficiency of Li was 97.1% without H2O2. And it reached to 99.4% with HCl - H2O2 ration being 10:1. A conclusion can be drawn from the experiment that H2O2 had a slight role in the leaching process of Li. In the leaching process for Hydrometallurgy, H2O2 was added as reductant to convert undissolved high valence ions to soluble low ones to promote the reaction process. Because of the absence of intermediate valence of Li, H2O2 can reduce Co(III) to Co (II) and thus facilitate the forward reaction. The result shows that the amount of H2O2 was sufficient for the complete dissolution of lithium, as further increasing the concentration of H2O2 did not show a significant effect in the recovery efficiency compared to consumption of expensive of lithium. Considering lower energy consumption and relatively better leaching efficiency, the suitable leaching agent was HCl without H2O2.",
        "properties": {
          "study_scope": "current_study",
          "graph_status": "active",
          "source_sample": "calcined anode powder",
          "evidence_span": "Fig. 9 shows the alteration of the leaching efficiency of Li as a function of HCl - H2O2 ratio in the range of 1:0–25:1 by using HCl concentration of 3 M, S/L of 1:50, leaching time of 90 min and leaching temperature of 80 ^∘C. It can be observed from Fig. 7 that the leaching efficiency of Li was 97.1% without H2O2. And it reached to 99.4% with HCl - H2O2 ration being 10:1. A conclusion can be drawn from the experiment that H2O2 had a slight role in the leaching process of Li. In the leaching process for Hydrometallurgy, H2O2 was added as reductant to convert undissolved high valence ions to soluble low ones to promote the reaction process. Because of the absence of intermediate valence of Li, H2O2 can reduce Co(III) to Co (II) and thus facilitate the forward reaction. The result shows that the amount of H2O2 was sufficient for the complete dissolution of lithium, as further increasing the concentration of H2O2 did not show a significant effect in the recovery efficiency compared to consumption of expensive of lithium. Considering lower energy consumption and relatively better leaching efficiency, the suitable leaching agent was HCl without H2O2."

At the first stage, the hydrolysis of lithium salt was dominated that caused leaching efficiency can reach 83.6% when only using deionized water as the leachate merely (see Eqs. (1)–(3)). After adding HCl, it increased from 84% to 98% with HCl concentration increasing from 2.5 to 3 M. This is because that the increasing acid concentration brings incremental amount of proton which can replace more Li+. On the other hand, when HCl was consumed at the solid liquid interphase, the concentration gradient of H+ between solid–liquid interface and solution was gradually increased. It leaded to a quicker diffusion velocity. Both of them can promote the leaching process and bring higher leaching efficiency. Henceforth, the leaching reaction reached equilibrium, and the leaching efficiency of lithium reached its maximum. This is because that the diffusion velocity would not change with further increase of HCl concentration, and the Li+ had been replaced sufficiently. From the figure, 3 M was employed as the optimum acid concentration."
          }
        },
        "confidence": 0.8
      },

          "chunk_id": "CHUNK_Leaching Lithium from the Anode Electrode Materials of Spent Lithium-Ion Batteries by Hydrochloric Acid (Hcl)_3.2. Leaching of lithium_de26a14a",
          "text": "3.2.1. Effect of hydrochloric acid concentration on leaching\nThe effect of HCl concentration was investigated with the concentration varied from 2.5 to 4 M at a leaching temperature of 80 \u0001C, S/L ratio of 1:50 g/mL, HCl–H O volume ratio of 10:1 and a leaching time of 90 min. The results are showed in Fig. 5, which demonstrated that the leaching efficiency of lithium was significantly influenced by the concentration of HCl, in other words, by the concentration of proton and ion diffusion velocity. The higher the concentration is, the faster the reaction will be.\nAs shown in Fig. 5, leaching efficiency was firstly increasing and then decreasing with the continuous increasing of acid concentration. At the first stage, the hydrolysis of lithium salt was dominated that caused leaching efficiency can reach 83.6% when only using deionized water as the leachate merely (see Eqs. (1)–(3)). After adding HCl, it increased from 84% to 98% with HCl concentration increasing from 2.5 to 3 M. This is because that the increasing acid concentration brings incremental amount of proton which can replace more Li+. On the other hand, when HCl was consumed at the solid liquid interphase, the concentration gradient of H+ between solid–liquid interface and solution was gradually increased. It leaded to a quicker diffusion velocity. Both of them can promote the leaching process and bring higher leaching efficiency. Henceforth, the leaching reaction reached equilibrium, and the leaching efficiency of lithium reached its maximum. This is because that the diffusion velocity would not change with further increase of HCl concentration, and the Li+ had been replaced sufficiently. From the figure, 3 M was employed as the optimum acid concentration.",

Effect of hydrochloric acid concentration on leaching\nThe effect of HCl concentration was investigated with the concentration varied from 2.5 to 4 M at a leaching temperature of 80 \u0001C, S/L ratio of 1:50 g/mL, HCl–H O volume ratio of 10:1 and a leaching time of 90 min. The higher the concentration is, the faster the reaction will be.\nAs shown in Fig. 5, leaching efficiency was firstly increasing and then decreasing with the continuous increasing of acid concentration. At the first stage, the hydrolysis of lithium salt was dominated that caused leaching efficiency can reach 83.6% when only using deionized water as the leachate merely (see Eqs. (1)–(3)). On the other hand, when HCl was consumed at the solid liquid interphase, the concentration gradient of H+ between solid–liquid interface and solution was gradually increased. It leaded to a quicker diffusion velocity. Both of them can promote the leaching process and bring higher leaching efficiency. Henceforth, the leaching reaction reached equilibrium, and the leaching efficiency of lithium reached its maximum. This is because that the diffusion velocity would not change with further increase of HCl concentration, and the Li+ had been replaced sufficiently. Effect of hydrochloric acid concentration on leaching\nThe effect of HCl concentration was investigated with the concentration varied from 2.5 to 4 M at a leaching temperature of 80 \u0001C, S/L ratio of 1:50 g/mL, HCl–H O volume ratio of 10:1 and a leaching time of 90 min. It leaded to a quicker diffusion velocity. Both of them can promote the leaching process and bring higher leaching efficiency. Henceforth, the leaching reaction reached equilibrium, and the leaching efficiency of lithium reached its maximum.           "text": "## 3.2.4. Effect of temperature on leaching\nThe effect of reaction temperature was studied with a range of temperature from 20 ^∘C to 100 ^∘𝖢. The leaching efficiency of Li increased from 89.7% to 99% with temperature from 20 ^∘C to 75 ^∘𝖢, then the curve maintain stable with the increasing temperature. Therefore, with the increasing of temperature, the ionization velocity of proton increased which can accelerate the reaction velocity.             "it increased from 84% to 98% with HCl concentration increasing from 2.5 to 3 M"
          ],
          "comparison_evidence": {
            "value": 84,
            "unit": "%",
            "treatment": "deionized water only",
            "evidence_span": "As shown in Fig. 5, leaching efficiency was firstly increasing and then decreasing with the continuous increasing of acid concentration. At the first stage, the hydrolysis of lithium salt was dominated that caused leaching efficiency can reach 83.6% when only using deionized water as the leachate merely (see Eqs. (1)–(3)). After adding HCl, it increased from 84% to 98% with HCl concentration increasing from 2.5 to 3 M. This is because that the increasing acid concentration brings incremental amount of proton which can replace more Li+. On the other hand, when HCl was consumed at the solid liquid interphase, the concentration gradient of H+ between solid–liquid interface and solution was gradually increased. It leaded to a quicker diffusion velocity. Both of them can promote the leaching process and bring higher leaching efficiency. Henceforth, the leaching reaction reached equilibrium, and the leaching efficiency of lithium reached its maximum. This is because that the diffusion velocity would not change with further increase of HCl concentration, and the Li+ had been replaced sufficiently. From the figure, 3 M was employed as the optimum acid concentration."
          }
        }

        "object_type": "Process",
        "evidence_span": "As described above, the main forms of Li in the anode active material are Li2CO3,, Li O, LiF, ROCO Li, CH OLi. Because some of them are water-soluble, such as ROCO2 Li CH3OLi Li2O,, in the experiment performed it has a high leaching efficiency of lithium (84%) only in deionized water. While others are almost insoluble in water and inlaid in the anode active materials, such as ROCO Li, LiF, and they will occur decomposition reaction in HCl solution. Therefore, the leaching reaction between anode materials and HCl solution is a multiple process (Aurbach et al., 2002; Jiangfeng et al., 2004): (1) the hydrolysis of lithium salt (2) double decomposition reaction between lithium salt and HCl solution.",
        "properties": {
          "study_scope": "current_study",
          "graph_status": "active",
          "source_sample": "calcined anode powder",
          "evidence_span": "As described above, the main forms of Li in the anode active material are Li2CO3,, Li O, LiF, ROCO Li, CH OLi. Because some of them are water-soluble, such as ROCO2 Li CH3OLi Li2O,, in the experiment performed it has a high leaching efficiency of lithium (84%) only in deionized water. While others are almost insoluble in water and inlaid in the anode active materials, such as ROCO Li, LiF, and they will occur decomposition reaction in HCl solution. Therefore, the leaching reaction between anode materials and HCl solution is a multiple process (Aurbach et al., 2002; Jiangfeng et al., 2004): (1) the hydrolysis of lithium salt (2) double decomposition reaction between lithium salt and HCl solution."
        }

      {
        "id": "REL_reviewed_input_Process_reviewed_8daa8098cc6a94",
        "subject": "Component_anode_active_material",
        "subject_name": "anode active material",
        "subject_type": "Component",
        "predicate": "PROCESSED_BY",
        "object": "Process_reviewed_8daa8098cc6a94",
        "object_name": "hydrochloric acid leaching of calcined anode powder without H2O2",
        "object_type": "Process",
        "evidence_span": "Fig. 9 shows the alteration of the leaching efficiency of Li as a function of HCl - H2O2 ratio in the range of 1:0–25:1 by using HCl concentration of 3 M, S/L of 1:50, leaching time of 90 min and leaching temperature of 80 ^∘C. It can be observed from Fig. 7 that the leaching efficiency of Li was 97.1% without H2O2. And it reached to 99.4% with HCl - H2O2 ration being 10:1. A conclusion can be drawn from the experiment that H2O2 had a slight role in the leaching process of Li. In the leaching process for Hydrometallurgy, H2O2 was added as reductant to convert undissolved high valence ions to soluble low ones to promote the reaction process. Because of the absence of intermediate valence of Li, H2O2 can reduce Co(III) to Co (II) and thus facilitate the forward reaction. The result shows that the amount of H2O2 was sufficient for the complete dissolution of lithium, as further increasing the concentration of H2O2 did not show a significant effect in the recovery efficiency compared to consumption of expensive of lithium. Considering lower energy consumption and relatively better leaching efficiency, the suitable leaching agent was HCl without H2O2.",
        "properties": {
          "study_scope": "current_study",
          "graph_status": "active",
          "source_sample": "calcined anode powder",
          "evidence_span": "Fig. 9 shows the alteration of the leaching efficiency of Li as a function of HCl - H2O2 ratio in the range of 1:0–25:1 by using HCl concentration of 3 M, S/L of 1:50, leaching time of 90 min and leaching temperature of 80 ^∘C. It can be observed from Fig. 7 that the leaching efficiency of Li was 97.1% without H2O2. And it reached to 99.4% with HCl - H2O2 ration being 10:1. A conclusion can be drawn from the experiment that H2O2 had a slight role in the leaching process of Li. In the leaching process for Hydrometallurgy, H2O2 was added as reductant to convert undissolved high valence ions to soluble low ones to promote the reaction process. Because of the absence of intermediate valence of Li, H2O2 can reduce Co(III) to Co (II) and thus facilitate the forward reaction. The result shows that the amount of H2O2 was sufficient for the complete dissolution of lithium, as further increasing the concentration of H2O2 did not show a significant effect in the recovery efficiency compared to consumption of expensive of lithium. Considering lower energy consumption and relatively better leaching efficiency, the suitable leaching agent was HCl without H2O2."

          "comparison_evidence": {
            "value": 84,
            "unit": "%",
            "treatment": "deionized water only",
            "evidence_span": "As shown in Fig. 5, leaching efficiency was firstly increasing and then decreasing with the continuous increasing of acid concentration. At the first stage, the hydrolysis of lithium salt was dominated that caused leaching efficiency can reach 83.6% when only using deionized water as the leachate merely (see Eqs. (1)–(3)). After adding HCl, it increased from 84% to 98% with HCl concentration increasing from 2.5 to 3 M. This is because that the increasing acid concentration brings incremental amount of proton which can replace more Li+. On the other hand, when HCl was consumed at the solid liquid interphase, the concentration gradient of H+ between solid–liquid interface and solution was gradually increased. It leaded to a quicker diffusion velocity. Both of them can promote the leaching process and bring higher leaching efficiency. Henceforth, the leaching reaction reached equilibrium, and the leaching efficiency of lithium reached its maximum. This is because that the diffusion velocity would not change with further increase of HCl concentration, and the Li+ had been replaced sufficiently. From the figure, 3 M was employed as the optimum acid concentration."
          }

        "evidence_span": "As described above, the main forms of Li in the anode active material are Li2CO3,, Li O, LiF, ROCO Li, CH OLi. Because some of them are water-soluble, such as ROCO2 Li CH3OLi Li2O,, in the experiment performed it has a high leaching efficiency of lithium (84%) only in deionized water. While others are almost insoluble in water and inlaid in the anode active materials, such as ROCO Li, LiF, and they will occur decomposition reaction in HCl solution. Therefore, the leaching reaction between anode materials and HCl solution is a multiple process (Aurbach et al., 2002; Jiangfeng et al., 2004): (1) the hydrolysis of lithium salt (2) double decomposition reaction between lithium salt and HCl solution.",
        "properties": {
          "study_scope": "current_study",
          "graph_status": "active",
          "source_sample": "calcined anode powder",
          "evidence_span": "As described above, the main forms of Li in the anode active material are Li2CO3,, Li O, LiF, ROCO Li, CH OLi. Because some of them are water-soluble, such as ROCO2 Li CH3OLi Li2O,, in the experiment performed it has a high leaching efficiency of lithium (84%) only in deionized water. While others are almost insoluble in water and inlaid in the anode active materials, such as ROCO Li, LiF, and they will occur decomposition reaction in HCl solution. Therefore, the leaching reaction between anode materials and HCl solution is a multiple process (Aurbach et al., 2002; Jiangfeng et al., 2004): (1) the hydrolysis of lithium salt (2) double decomposition reaction between lithium salt and HCl solution."
        }

        "object_type": "Process",
        "evidence_span": "Fig. 9 shows the alteration of the leaching efficiency of Li as a function of HCl - H2O2 ratio in the range of 1:0–25:1 by using HCl concentration of 3 M, S/L of 1:50, leaching time of 90 min and leaching temperature of 80 ^∘C. It can be observed from Fig. 7 that the leaching efficiency of Li was 97.1% without H2O2. And it reached to 99.4% with HCl - H2O2 ration being 10:1. A conclusion can be drawn from the experiment that H2O2 had a slight role in the leaching process of Li. In the leaching process for Hydrometallurgy, H2O2 was added as reductant to convert undissolved high valence ions to soluble low ones to promote the reaction process. Because of the absence of intermediate valence of Li, H2O2 can reduce Co(III) to Co (II) and thus facilitate the forward reaction. The result shows that the amount of H2O2 was sufficient for the complete dissolution of lithium, as further increasing the concentration of H2O2 did not show a significant effect in the recovery efficiency compared to consumption of expensive of lithium. Considering lower energy consumption and relatively better leaching efficiency, the suitable leaching agent was HCl without H2O2.",
        "properties": {
          "study_scope": "current_study",

It can be observed from Fig. 7 that the leaching efficiency of Li was 97.1% without H2O2. And it reached to 99.4% with HCl - H2O2 ration being 10:1. A conclusion can be drawn from the experiment that H2O2 had a slight role in the leaching process of Li. In the leaching process for Hydrometallurgy, H2O2 was added as reductant to convert undissolved high valence ions to soluble low ones to promote the reaction process. Because of the absence of intermediate valence of Li, H2O2 can reduce Co(III) to Co (II) and thus facilitate the forward reaction. The result shows that the amount of H2O2 was sufficient for the complete dissolution of lithium, as further increasing the concentration of H2O2 did not show a significant effect in the recovery efficiency compared to consumption of expensive of lithium. Considering lower energy consumption and relatively better leaching efficiency, the suitable leaching agent was HCl without H2O2."
